> [!TLDR]
> A broken weak-isospin and hypercharge gauge symmetry gives masses to the weak bosons and electron while preserving a massless photon.

<!-- PDF page 134; printed page 120 -->

The $\mathrm{SU}(2)_L\otimes\mathrm{U}(1)_Y$ gauge theory that has emerged as “standard” is the result of a long development and many ingenious contributions. We first consider the electroweak theory in its purely leptonic form, which exhibits the motivation and the principal features.

We begin by designating the spectrum of fundamental fermions of the theory. It suffices for the moment to include only the electron and its neutrino, which form a left-handed “weak-isospin” doublet,

$$
\mathsf L_e\equiv\begin{pmatrix}\nu\\e\end{pmatrix}_L, \tag{6.3.1}
$$

where the left-handed states are

$$
\begin{aligned}
\nu_L&=\tfrac12(1-\gamma_5)\nu\\
e_L&=\tfrac12(1-\gamma_5)e.
\end{aligned} \tag{6.3.2}
$$

The helicity of electron neutrinos emitted in charged-current interactions was determined to be negative in a classic experiment [11] that inferred the longitudinal polarization of the recoil nucleus in the reaction

$$
\begin{aligned}
e^-\,{}^{152}\mathrm{Eu}^{\mathrm m}(J=0)&\rightarrow{}^{152}\mathrm{Sm}^*(J=1)\,\nu_e\\
&\hspace{3.4em}\hookrightarrow\gamma\,{}^{152}\mathrm{Sm}.
\end{aligned} \tag{6.3.3}
$$

A measurement of the photon polarization determines the neutrino helicity by angular momentum conservation. The electron neutrino is known to have a very small mass ($\lesssim2\,\unit{eV}$), and all neutrino masses are too small to have been determined by kinematic constraints (Ref. [4]). It is convenient to idealize the neutrino as exactly massless,* in which case the right-handed state

$$
\nu_R=\tfrac12(1+\gamma_5)\nu \tag{6.3.4}
$$

does not exist. Thus we designate only one right-handed lepton,

$$
\mathsf R_e=e_R=\tfrac12(1+\gamma_5)e, \tag{6.3.5}
$$

which is a weak-isospin singlet. This completes a specification of the weak charged currents. To incorporate electromagnetism, we define a “weak hypercharge,” $Y$. Requiring that the Gell-Mann–Nishijima relation for the electric charge,

$$
\electric{Q}=I_3+\tfrac12Y, \tag{6.3.6}
$$

> [!note]
> * We will revisit the question of neutrino masses and mixings in section 6.7.

<!-- PDF page 135; printed page 121 -->

be satisfied leads to the assignments

$$
\begin{aligned}
Y_L&=-1,\\
Y_R&=-2.
\end{aligned} \tag{6.3.7}
$$

By construction, the weak-isospin projection $I_3$ and the weak hypercharge $Y$ are commuting observables,

$$
[I_3,Y]=0. \tag{6.3.8}
$$

We now take the (product) group of transformations generated by $I$ and $Y$ to be the gauge group $\mathrm{SU}(2)_L\otimes\mathrm{U}(1)_Y$ of the theory. To construct the theory, we introduce the gauge bosons

$$
\begin{aligned}
b_\mu^1,b_\mu^2,b_\mu^3&\qquad\text{for }\mathrm{SU}(2)_L,\\
\mathcal A_\mu&\qquad\text{for }\mathrm{U}(1)_Y.
\end{aligned}
$$

Evidently the Lagrangian for the theory may be written as

$$
\mathcal L=\mathcal L_{\mathrm{gauge}}+\mathcal L_{\mathrm{leptons}} \tag{6.3.9}
$$

where the kinetic term for the gauge fields is

$$
\mathcal L_{\mathrm{gauge}}=-\tfrac14F_{\mu\nu}^lF^{l\mu\nu}-\tfrac14f_{\mu\nu}f^{\mu\nu}, \tag{6.3.10}
$$

and the field-strength tensors are

$$
F_{\mu\nu}^l=\partial_\nu b_\mu^l-\partial_\mu b_\nu^l+g\epsilon_{jkl}b_\mu^jb_\nu^k \tag{6.3.11}
$$

for the $\mathrm{SU}(2)_L$ gauge fields [cf. (4.2.37)] and

$$
f_{\mu\nu}=\partial_\nu\mathcal A_\mu-\partial_\mu\mathcal A_\nu \tag{6.3.12}
$$

for the $\mathrm{U}(1)_Y$ gauge field [cf. (3.2.10)]. The matter term is

$$
\mathcal L_{\mathrm{leptons}}=\bar{\mathsf R}i\gamma^\mu\left(\partial_\mu+\frac{ig'}2\mathcal A_\mu Y\right)\mathsf R+\bar{\mathsf L}i\gamma^\mu\left(\partial_\mu+\frac{ig'}2\mathcal A_\mu Y+\frac{ig}2\boldsymbol\tau\cdot\mathbf b_\mu\right)\mathsf L. \tag{6.3.13}
$$

The coupling of the weak-isospin group $\mathrm{SU}(2)_L$ is called $g$, as in the Yang–Mills theory of chapter 4, and the coupling constant for the weak-hypercharge group $\mathrm{U}(1)_Y$ is denoted as $g'/2$, the factor $\tfrac12$ being chosen to simplify later expressions.

The theory of weak and electromagnetic interactions described by the Lagrangian (6.3.9) is not a satisfactory one for two immediately obvious reasons. It contains four massless gauge bosons $(b^1,b^2,b^3,\mathcal A)$, whereas nature has but one, the photon. In addition, the expression (6.3.13) represents a massless electron; it lacks the $-\mechanical{m_e}\bar ee$ term of the QED Lagrangian (2.2.7), and for good reason. A fermion

<!-- PDF page 136; printed page 122 -->

mass term links left-handed and right-handed components:

$$
\bar ee=\tfrac12\bar e(1-\gamma_5)e+\tfrac12\bar e(1+\gamma_5)e=\bar e_Re_L+\bar e_Le_R. \tag{6.3.14}
$$

The left-handed and right-handed components of the electron transform differently under $\mathrm{SU}(2)_L$ and $\mathrm{U}(1)_Y$, so an explicit fermion mass term would break the $\mathrm{SU}(2)_L\otimes\mathrm{U}(1)_Y$ gauge invariance of the theory: a mass term is forbidden. Our task is to modify the theory so that there will remain only a single conserved quantity (the electric charge) corresponding to one massless gauge boson (the photon), and the electron will acquire mass.

To accomplish these things, we introduce a complex doublet of scalar fields,

$$
\phi\equiv\begin{pmatrix}\phi^+\\\phi^0\end{pmatrix}, \tag{6.3.15}
$$

that transforms as an $\mathrm{SU}(2)_L$ doublet and must, therefore, have weak hypercharge

$$
Y_\phi=+1, \tag{6.3.16}
$$

by virtue of the Gell-Mann–Nishijima relation (6.3.6). We add to the Lagrangian a term

$$
\mathcal L_{\mathrm{scalar}}=(\mathcal D^\mu\phi)^\dagger(\mathcal D_\mu\phi)-\energy{V}(\phi^\dagger\phi), \tag{6.3.17}
$$

where the gauge-covariant derivative is

$$
\mathcal D_\mu=\partial_\mu+\frac{ig'}2\mathcal A_\mu Y+\frac{ig}2\boldsymbol\tau\cdot\mathbf b_\mu \tag{6.3.18}
$$

and, as usual, the potential is

$$
\energy{V}(\phi^\dagger\phi)=\mechanical{\mu}^2(\phi^\dagger\phi)+|\lambda|(\phi^\dagger\phi)^2. \tag{6.3.19}
$$

We are also free to add an interaction term, which involves Yukawa couplings of the scalars to the fermions,

$$
\mathcal L_{\mathrm{Yukawa}}=-\zeta_e[\bar{\mathsf R}(\phi^\dagger\mathsf L)+(\bar{\mathsf L}\phi)\mathsf R], \tag{6.3.20}
$$

a Lorentz scalar symmetric under local $\mathrm{SU}(2)_L\otimes\mathrm{U}(1)_Y$ rotations.

Now let us imagine that $\mechanical{\mu}^2<0$ and consider the consequences of spontaneous symmetry breaking. We choose as the vacuum expectation value of the scalar field

$$
\langle\phi\rangle_0=\begin{pmatrix}0\\v/\sqrt2\end{pmatrix}, \tag{6.3.21}
$$

where $v=\sqrt{-\mechanical{\mu}^2/|\lambda|}$, which breaks both $\mathrm{SU}(2)_L$ and $\mathrm{U}(1)_Y$ symmetries but preserves an invariance under the $\mathrm{U}(1)_{\mathrm{EM}}$ symmetry generated by the electric charge operator. Recall that a (would-be) Goldstone boson is associated with every

<!-- PDF page 137; printed page 123 -->

generator of the gauge group that does not leave the vacuum invariant. The vacuum is left invariant by a generator $\mathcal G$ if

$$
e^{i\alpha\mathcal G}\langle\phi\rangle_0=\langle\phi\rangle_0. \tag{6.3.22}
$$

For an infinitesimal transformation, (6.3.22) becomes

$$
(1+i\alpha\mathcal G)\langle\phi\rangle_0=\langle\phi\rangle_0, \tag{6.3.23}
$$

so that the condition for $\mathcal G$ to leave the vacuum invariant is simply

$$
\mathcal G\langle\phi\rangle_0=0. \tag{6.3.24}
$$

We easily compute that

$$
\begin{aligned}
\tau_1\langle\phi\rangle_0&=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\1&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}0\\v/\sqrt2\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}v/\sqrt2\\0\end{pmatrix}\ne0,\quad\text{Broken!}\\[1em]
\tau_2\langle\phi\rangle_0&=\begin{pmatrix}0&-i\\i&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}0\\v/\sqrt2\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}-iv/\sqrt2\\0\end{pmatrix}\ne0,\quad\text{Broken!}\\[1em]
\tau_3\langle\phi\rangle_0&=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\0&-1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}0\\v/\sqrt2\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}0\\-v/\sqrt2\end{pmatrix}\ne0,\quad\text{Broken!}\\[1em]
Y\langle\phi\rangle_0&=Y_\phi\langle\phi\rangle_0=+1\langle\phi\rangle_0=\begin{pmatrix}0\\v/\sqrt2\end{pmatrix}\ne0,\quad\text{Broken!}
\end{aligned} \tag{6.3.25}
$$

However, if we examine the effect of the electric charge operator $\electric{Q}$ on the (electrically neutral) vacuum state, we find that

$$
\begin{aligned}
\electric{Q}\langle\phi\rangle_0&=\tfrac12(\tau_3+Y)\langle\phi\rangle_0=\tfrac12\begin{pmatrix}Y_\phi+1&0\\0&Y_\phi-1\end{pmatrix}\langle\phi\rangle_0\\[1em]
&=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\0&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}0\\v/\sqrt2\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}0\\0\end{pmatrix},\quad\textit{Unbroken!}
\end{aligned} \tag{6.3.26}
$$

This is promising! All the original four generators are broken, but the linear combination corresponding to electric charge is not. The photon will, therefore, remain massless, whereas three other gauge bosons will acquire mass.

We next expand the Lagrangian about the minimum of the Higgs potential, $\energy{V}$, by writing

$$
\phi=\exp\left(\frac{i\boldsymbol\zeta\cdot\boldsymbol\tau}{2v}\right)\begin{pmatrix}0\\(v+\eta)/\sqrt2\end{pmatrix} \tag{6.3.27}
$$

<!-- PDF page 138; printed page 124 -->

and transforming at once to U-gauge:

$$
\phi\rightarrow\phi'=\exp\left(-i\frac{\boldsymbol\zeta\cdot\boldsymbol\tau}{2v}\right)\phi=\begin{pmatrix}0\\(v+\eta)/\sqrt2\end{pmatrix}, \tag{6.3.28}
$$

$$
\boldsymbol\tau\cdot\mathbf b_\mu\rightarrow\boldsymbol\tau\cdot\mathbf b'_\mu, \tag{6.3.29}
$$

$$
\mathcal A_\mu\rightarrow\mathcal A_\mu, \tag{6.3.30}
$$

$$
\mathsf R\rightarrow\mathsf R, \tag{6.3.31}
$$

$$
\mathsf L\rightarrow\mathsf L'=\exp\left(-i\frac{\boldsymbol\zeta\cdot\boldsymbol\tau}{2v}\right)\mathsf L. \tag{6.3.32}
$$

Had we followed literally the procedure of section 5.5, we would have replaced the generator $\tau_3$ in the expansion (6.3.27) of $\phi$ about $\langle\phi\rangle_0$ by the combination $K=\tfrac12(\tau_3-Y)$ orthogonal to $\electric{Q}$, which is, strictly speaking, the third broken generator. However, because $\electric{Q}$ leaves the vacuum invariant and $\tau_3=K+\electric{Q}$, the effect is the same.

We may now reëxpress the Lagrangian in terms of the U-gauge fields (6.3.28)–(6.3.32), omitting primes to avoid notational clutter, and investigate the consequences of spontaneous symmetry breaking.

The scalar term in the Lagrangian now reads

$$
\begin{aligned}
\mathcal L_{\mathrm{scalar}}&=\frac{v^2}8\left[g^2|b_\mu^1-ib_\mu^2|^2+(g'\mathcal A_\mu-gb_\mu^3)^2\right]\\
&\quad+\frac12[(\partial^\mu\eta)(\partial_\mu\eta)+2\mechanical{\mu}^2\eta^2]+\cdots
\end{aligned} \tag{6.3.33}
$$

plus interaction terms. If we define the charged gauge fields

$$
W_\mu^\pm\equiv\frac{b_\mu^1\mp ib_\mu^2}{\sqrt2}, \tag{6.3.34}
$$

the term proportional to $g^2v^2$ is recognizable as a mass term for the charged vector bosons:

$$
\frac{g^2v^2}2(|W_\mu^+|^2+|W_\mu^-|^2), \tag{6.3.35}
$$

corresponding to the charged intermediate boson masses

$$
\mechanical{M_{W^\pm}}=\frac{gv}2. \tag{6.3.36}
$$

<!-- PDF page 139; printed page 125 -->

Defining the orthogonal combinations

$$
Z_\mu=\frac{-g'\mathcal A_\mu+gb_\mu^3}{\sqrt{g^2+g'^2}} \tag{6.3.37}
$$

and

$$
\magnetic{A_\mu}=\frac{g\mathcal A_\mu+g'b_\mu^3}{\sqrt{g^2+g'^2}}, \tag{6.3.38}
$$

we find that the neutral intermediate boson $Z^0$ has acquired a mass

$$
\mechanical{M_{Z^0}}=\sqrt{g^2+g'^2}\,v/2=\mechanical{M_W}\sqrt{1+g'^2/g^2}, \tag{6.3.39}
$$

and that the field $\magnetic{A_\mu}$ remains as a massless gauge boson corresponding to the surviving $\exp[i\electric{Q}\alpha(x)]$ symmetry.

Examining the second line of (6.3.33), we see at once [cf. (2.2.1)] that the $\eta$ field has acquired a $(\text{mass})^2$,

$$
\mechanical{M_H}^2=-2\mechanical{\mu}^2>0; \tag{6.3.40}
$$

it is the physical Higgs boson.

Finally, the Yukawa term in the Lagrangian has become

$$
\begin{aligned}
\mathcal L_{\mathrm{Yukawa}}&=-\zeta_e\frac{(v+\eta)}{\sqrt2}(\bar e_Re_L+\bar e_Le_R)\\
&=-\frac{\zeta_ev}{\sqrt2}\bar ee-\frac{\zeta_e\eta}{\sqrt2}\bar ee,
\end{aligned} \tag{6.3.41}
$$

so the electron has acquired a mass

$$
\mechanical{m_e}=\frac{\zeta_ev}{\sqrt2}. \tag{6.3.42}
$$

As the originally massless nucleons in the $\sigma$ model (cf. §5.4) gained mass from their interactions with the massive scalar $\sigma$, the electron and other fermions acquire mass from their interactions with the $\eta$ field. We see that we have achieved, at least schematically, the desired particle content—plus a massive Higgs scalar we did not request.

Do the interactions correspond to those in nature? The interactions among the gauge bosons and leptons may be read from (6.3.13), $\mathcal L_{\mathrm{leptons}}$. For the charged gauge bosons, we find

$$
\begin{aligned}
\mathcal L_{W:\ell}&=-\frac{g}{\sqrt2}(\bar\nu_L\gamma^\mu e_LW_\mu^++\bar e_L\gamma^\mu\nu_LW_\mu^-)\\
&=-\frac{g}{2\sqrt2}[\bar\nu\gamma^\mu(1-\gamma_5)eW_\mu^++\bar e\gamma^\mu(1-\gamma_5)\nu W_\mu^-],
\end{aligned} \tag{6.3.43}
$$

<!-- PDF page 140; printed page 126 -->

which reproduces the low-energy phenomenology of the ad hoc intermediate boson model of section 6.2, provided that we identify the coupling constants as

$$
\frac{g^2}8=\frac{G_F\mechanical{M_W}^2}{\sqrt2}. \tag{6.3.44}
$$

With the aid of the expression (6.3.35) for the intermediate-boson mass, we find that the vacuum expectation value parameter $v$ is now determined as

$$
v=(G_F\sqrt2)^{-1/2}\approx246\,\unit{GeV}, \tag{6.3.45}
$$

so that the vacuum expectation value of the scalar field is

$$
\langle\phi^0\rangle_0=(G_F\sqrt8)^{-1/2}\approx174\,\unit{GeV}. \tag{6.3.46}
$$

Similarly, the neutral-gauge-boson couplings to leptons are given by

$$
\begin{aligned}
\mathcal L_{0:\ell}&=\frac{gg'}{\sqrt{g^2+g'^2}}\bar e\gamma^\mu e\magnetic{A_\mu}\\
&\quad-\frac{\sqrt{g^2+g'^2}}2\bar\nu_L\gamma^\mu\nu_L Z_\mu\\
&\quad+\frac1{\sqrt{g^2+g'^2}}\left[-g'^2\bar e_R\gamma^\mu e_R+\frac{(g^2-g'^2)}2\bar e_L\gamma^\mu e_L\right]Z_\mu.
\end{aligned} \tag{6.3.47}
$$

Therefore, we may indeed identify $\magnetic{A_\mu}$ as the photon, provided that we set

$$
\frac{gg'}{\sqrt{g^2+g'^2}}=\electric{e}. \tag{6.3.48}
$$

If, in analogy with the fine structure constant of electromagnetism, $\alpha\equiv\electric{e}^2/4\pi\mechanical{\hbar}\mechanical{c}$, we define $\alpha_2\equiv g^2/4\pi\mechanical{\hbar}\mechanical{c}$ and $\alpha_Y\equiv g'^2/4\pi\mechanical{\hbar}\mechanical{c}$, we may recast (6.3.48) as

$$
1/\alpha=1/\alpha_Y+1/\alpha_2. \tag{6.3.49}
$$

It is convenient to introduce a weak mixing angle $\theta_W$ to parametrize the mixing of the neutral gauge bosons. With the definition

$$
g'\equiv g\tan\theta_W, \tag{6.3.50}
$$

whence

$$
\sqrt{g^2+g'^2}=\frac{g}{\cos\theta_W}, \tag{6.3.51}
$$

<!-- PDF page 141; printed page 127 -->

(6.3.37) and (6.3.38) may be rewritten as

$$
Z_\mu=-\mathcal A_\mu\sin\theta_W+b_\mu^3\cos\theta_W, \tag{6.3.52}
$$

$$
\magnetic{A_\mu}=\mathcal A_\mu\cos\theta_W+b_\mu^3\sin\theta_W, \tag{6.3.53}
$$

which may be inverted to yield

$$
\mathcal A_\mu=\magnetic{A_\mu}\cos\theta_W-Z_\mu\sin\theta_W, \tag{6.3.54}
$$

$$
b_\mu^3=\magnetic{A_\mu}\sin\theta_W+Z_\mu\cos\theta_W. \tag{6.3.55}
$$

In view of the identification (6.3.50), the coupling constants of the $\mathrm{SU}(2)_L$ and $\mathrm{U}(1)_Y$ gauge groups may be expressed as

$$
g=\frac{\electric{e}}{\sin\theta_W}\ge\electric{e}, \tag{6.3.56}
$$

$$
g'=\frac{\electric{e}}{\cos\theta_W}\ge\electric{e}, \tag{6.3.57}
$$

indicating that the previously disparate strengths of the weak and electromagnetic interactions are now related through a single parameter.

Taken together, the coupling constant identifications lead to

$$
\begin{aligned}
\mechanical{M_W}^2&=\frac{g^2}{4G_F\sqrt2}=\frac{\electric{e}^2}{4G_F\sqrt2\sin^2\theta_W}\\
&=\frac{\pi\alpha}{G_F\sqrt2\sin^2\theta_W}\\
&\approx\frac{(37.3\,\unit{GeV})^2}{\sin^2\theta_W},
\end{aligned} \tag{6.3.58}
$$

and

$$
\mechanical{M_Z}^2=\frac{\mechanical{M_W}^2}{\cos^2\theta_W}\ge\mechanical{M_W}^2. \tag{6.3.59}
$$

The feebleness of the weak interactions at low energies is thus laid to the large mass of the intermediate bosons and not to an intrinsically small coupling constant.

Note that the dimensionless coupling constant that endowed the electron with mass in (6.3.42) is both small,

$$
\zeta_e=\frac{\mechanical{m_e}\sqrt2}{v}=2^{3/4}\mechanical{m_e}G_F^{1/2}\approx3\times10^{-6}, \tag{6.3.60}
$$

and arbitrary.

<!-- PDF page 142; printed page 128 -->

It is convenient to rewrite the interaction Lagrangian (6.3.47) in terms of the weak mixing angle as

$$
\begin{aligned}
\mathcal L_{0:\ell}&=\electric{e}\bar e\gamma^\mu e\magnetic{A_\mu}-\frac{g}{2\cos\theta_W}\bar\nu_L\gamma^\mu\nu_L Z_\mu\\
&\quad-\frac{g}{2\cos\theta_W}[2\sin^2\theta_W\bar e_R\gamma^\mu e_R Z_\mu+(2\sin^2\theta_W-1)\bar e_L\gamma^\mu e_L Z_\mu]\\
&=\electric{e}\bar e\gamma^\mu e\magnetic{A_\mu}-\frac1{\sqrt2}\left(\frac{G_F\mechanical{M_Z}^2}{\sqrt2}\right)^{1/2}\bar\nu\gamma^\mu(1-\gamma_5)\nu Z_\mu\\
&\quad-\frac1{\sqrt2}\left(\frac{G_F\mechanical{M_Z}^2}{\sqrt2}\right)^{1/2}[2\sin^2\theta_W\bar e\gamma^\mu(1+\gamma_5)eZ_\mu\\
&\qquad+(2\sin^2\theta_W-1)\bar e\gamma^\mu(1-\gamma_5)eZ_\mu],
\end{aligned} \tag{6.3.61}
$$

from which Feynman rules for the elementary vertices may readily be deduced. These are shown in figure 6.12.

The low-energy phenomenology of the model has been constructed to reproduce that of the effective Lagrangian description, which has been the subject of sections 6.1 and 6.2. There are several novel elements as well: neutral weak-current phenomena (to be studied in section 6.4), some definite predictions for the properties of gauge bosons, and good high-energy behavior—indeed, renormalizability. Let us look briefly at the last two of these.

In the $\mathrm{SU}(2)_L\otimes\mathrm{U}(1)_Y$ model, the properties of gauge bosons are correlated with those of the neutral-current interactions through the weak mixing angle $\theta_W$. The masses have already been expressed in terms of $\theta_W$ in (6.3.58) and (6.3.59). They are also plotted in the top pane of figure 6.13. The expression (6.2.20) for the partial decay rate of $W^\pm$ continues to hold because there has been no change in the charged-current phenomenology. By combining (6.3.58) and (6.2.20) we obtain the useful expression

$$
\Gamma(W^-\rightarrow e^-\bar\nu_e)\approx\frac{23\,\unit{MeV}}{\sin^3\theta_W}. \tag{6.3.62}
$$

The leptonic decay rates of the neutral gauge boson may be computed almost by transcription. Evidently,

$$
|\mathcal M(Z^0\rightarrow\nu\bar\nu)|^2=\frac12\cdot\frac{\mechanical{M_Z}^2}{\mechanical{M_W}^2}|\mathcal M(W^-\rightarrow e^-\bar\nu)|^2, \tag{6.3.63}
$$

neglecting the electron mass, so that

$$
\Gamma(Z^0\rightarrow\nu\bar\nu)=\frac{G_F\mechanical{M_Z}^3}{12\pi\sqrt2}\approx\frac{11.4\,\unit{MeV}}{(\sin\theta_W\cos\theta_W)^3}. \tag{6.3.64}
$$

<!-- PDF page 143; printed page 129 -->

![[gauge-theories-fig-6-12-lepton-gauge-vertices.svg|**Fig. 6.12** Feynman rules for lepton interactions in the $\mathrm{SU}(2)_L\otimes\mathrm{U}(1)_Y$ electroweak theory.]]

The factors shown beside the four source vertices are:

(a) $eeA_\mu$:

$$
-i\electric{q}\gamma_\mu=i\electric{e}\gamma_\mu.
$$

(b) $\nu\nu Z_\mu$:

$$
\frac{-ig}{4\cos\theta_W}\gamma_\mu(1-\gamma_5)=\frac{-i}{\sqrt2}\left(\frac{G_F\mechanical{M_Z}^2}{\sqrt2}\right)^{1/2}\gamma_\mu(1-\gamma_5).
$$

(c) $eeZ_\mu$:

$$
\begin{aligned}
&\frac{-ig}{4\cos\theta_W}\gamma_\mu[L_e(1-\gamma_5)+R_e(1+\gamma_5)]\\
&\quad=\frac{-i}{\sqrt2}\left(\frac{G_F\mechanical{M_Z}^2}{\sqrt2}\right)^{1/2}\gamma_\mu[L_e(1-\gamma_5)+R_e(1+\gamma_5)],\\
&\quad(L_e=2\sin^2\theta_W-1;\ R_e=2\sin^2\theta_W).
\end{aligned}
$$

(d) $\nu eW^+$:

$$
\frac{-ig}{2\sqrt2}\gamma_\mu(1-\gamma_5)=-i\left(\frac{G_F\mechanical{M_W}^2}{\sqrt2}\right)^{1/2}\gamma_\mu(1-\gamma_5).
$$

Similarly, we find for the decays into charged leptons

$$
\begin{aligned}
\Gamma(Z^0\rightarrow e^+e^-)&=\Gamma(Z^0\rightarrow\nu\bar\nu)[L_e^2+R_e^2]\\
&=\Gamma(Z^0\rightarrow\nu\bar\nu)[(2\sin^2\theta_W-1)^2+(2\sin^2\theta_W)^2],
\end{aligned} \tag{6.3.65}
$$

where the two terms correspond to the contributions of left-handed and right-handed chiral couplings defined in figure 6.12. Our expectations for the partial decay rates are shown in the lower pane of figure 6.13.

**Fig. 6.13** Masses (upper pane) and leptonic decay widths (lower pane) of the intermediate bosons $W^\pm$ and $Z^0$ in the $\mathrm{SU}(2)_L\otimes\mathrm{U}(1)_Y$ electroweak theory, as functions of the weak mixing parameter $\sin^2\theta_W$.

We shall examine the predictions of the electroweak theory in some detail in section 6.4 and chapter 7, but it is worth anticipating some of the consequences right away. The observed mass of the $Z$-boson (Ref. [4]), $\mechanical{M_Z}=(91.1876\pm0.0021)\,\unit{GeV}$, and neutral current studies point to a value of the weak-mixing parameter $\sin^2\theta_W\approx0.2$. Using our lowest-order expression (6.3.59) for $\mechanical{M_Z}$ to fix $\sin^2\theta_W=0.2122$, we find (measured values in brackets)

$$
\begin{aligned}
\mechanical{M_W}&\approx81\,\unit{GeV}\quad[80.4\,\unit{GeV}],\\
\Gamma(W\rightarrow e\nu)&\approx232\,\unit{MeV}\quad[224\,\unit{MeV}],
\end{aligned} \tag{6.3.66}
$$

and

$$
\begin{aligned}
\Gamma(Z\rightarrow\nu\bar\nu)&\approx166\,\unit{MeV}\quad[166\,\unit{MeV}],\\
\Gamma(Z\rightarrow e^+e^-)&\approx85\,\unit{MeV}\quad[84\,\unit{MeV}],
\end{aligned} \tag{6.3.67}
$$

<!-- PDF page 145; printed page 131 -->

![[gauge-theories-fig-6-14-trilinear-gauge-vertices.svg|**Fig. 6.14** Trilinear interactions among electroweak gauge bosons. Recall that $g=\electric{e}/\sin\theta_W$, $g'=\electric{e}/\cos\theta_W=g\tan\theta_W$. Indicated charges and momenta flow toward the vertex.]]

The factors shown beside the two source vertices are:

(a) $W_\nu^+(\mechanical{q})W_\lambda^-(\mechanical{k})\magnetic{A_\mu}(\mechanical{p})$:

$$
i\electric{e}[g_{\mu\nu}(\mechanical{p}-\mechanical{q})_\lambda+g_{\lambda\mu}(\mechanical{k}-\mechanical{p})_\nu+g_{\nu\lambda}(\mechanical{q}-\mechanical{k})_\mu].
$$

(b) $W_\nu^+(\mechanical{q})W_\lambda^-(\mechanical{k})Z_\mu(\mechanical{p})$:

$$
ig\cos\theta_W[g_{\mu\nu}(\mechanical{p}-\mechanical{q})_\lambda+g_{\lambda\mu}(\mechanical{k}-\mechanical{p})_\nu+g_{\nu\lambda}(\mechanical{q}-\mechanical{k})_\mu].
$$

which shows very promising agreement between theory and experiment. (Another choice [cf. (9.3.22)] would have been to fix the weak mixing parameter from the ratio $\mechanical{M_W}^2/\mechanical{M_Z}^2$, which yields $\sin^2\theta_W=0.2226$.) We will complete the portrait of the electroweak gauge bosons in section 7.2, after incorporating quarks into the theory.

As a first look at the high-energy properties of the theory, let us reconsider the reaction

$$
\nu_e\bar\nu_e\rightarrow W_0^+W_0^-, \tag{6.2.25}
$$

the comportment of which was symptomatic of the diseases of the ad hoc intermediate-boson theory. To do so, we must determine the Feynman rules for interactions among the gauge bosons. The trilinear and quadrilinear entries in the kinetic term (6.3.10) of the Lagrangian $\mathcal L_{\mathrm{gauge}}$ give rise to the vertices shown in figures 6.14 and 6.15. The appearance of the $Z^0W^+W^-$ vertex, together with the $Z^0\nu\bar\nu$ vertex already discussed, signals that there will be a new contribution to the amplitude for the reaction $\nu_e\bar\nu_e\rightarrow W^+W^-$: the $s$-channel $Z^0$-exchange graph shown in figure 6.16. Its contribution to the amplitude is

$$
\begin{aligned}
\mathcal M_Z&=\frac{-ig^2}{4(\mechanical{S}^2-\mechanical{M_Z}^2)}\bar v(\nu,\mechanical{q_2})\gamma_\mu(1-\gamma_5)u(\nu,\mechanical{q_1})\left(g^{\mu\nu}-\frac{\mechanical{S^\mu}\mechanical{S^\nu}}{\mechanical{M_Z}^2}\right)\\
&\quad\times\epsilon_+^{*\alpha}\epsilon_-^{*\beta}[g_{\alpha\beta}(\mechanical{k_-}-\mechanical{k_+})_\nu+g_{\alpha\nu}[(\mechanical{k_+}+\mechanical{S})_\beta-g_{\beta\nu}(\mechanical{k_-}+\mechanical{S})_\alpha],
\end{aligned} \tag{6.3.68}
$$

where

$$
\mechanical{S}\equiv\mechanical{q_1}+\mechanical{q_2}=\mechanical{k_+}+\mechanical{k_-} \tag{6.3.69}
$$

<!-- PDF page 146; printed page 132 -->

![[gauge-theories-fig-6-15-quadrilinear-gauge-vertices.svg|**Fig. 6.15** Quadrilinear interactions among electroweak gauge bosons. Recall that $g=\electric{e}/\sin\theta_W$ and $g'=\electric{e}/\cos\theta_W=g\tan\theta_W$. Indicated charges flow toward the vertex.]]

The factors shown beside the four source vertices are:

(a) $W_\mu^+W_\nu^-\gamma_\kappa\gamma_\lambda$:

$$
-i\electric{e}^2(2g_{\kappa\lambda}g_{\mu\nu}-g_{\kappa\mu}g_{\lambda\nu}-g_{\kappa\nu}g_{\lambda\mu}).
$$

(b) $W_\mu^+W_\nu^-\gamma_\kappa Z_\lambda$:

$$
-i\electric{e}g\cos\theta_W(2g_{\kappa\lambda}g_{\mu\nu}-g_{\kappa\mu}g_{\lambda\nu}-g_{\kappa\nu}g_{\lambda\mu}).
$$

(c) $W_\mu^+W_\nu^-Z_\kappa Z_\lambda$:

$$
-ig^2\cos^2\theta_W(2g_{\kappa\lambda}g_{\mu\nu}-g_{\kappa\mu}g_{\lambda\nu}-g_{\kappa\nu}g_{\lambda\mu}).
$$

(d) $W_\mu^+W_\nu^-W_\kappa^+W_\lambda^-$:

$$
+ig^2(2g_{\kappa\mu}g_{\lambda\nu}-g_{\kappa\lambda}g_{\mu\nu}-g_{\kappa\nu}g_{\lambda\mu}).
$$

and the other kinematical quantities have been defined in (6.2.28)–(6.2.31). The $\mechanical{S^\mu}\mechanical{S^\nu}$ term is impotent between massless spinors. Thus we have only to evaluate

$$
\begin{aligned}
\mathcal M_Z&=\frac{ig^2}{4(\mechanical{S}^2-\mechanical{M_Z}^2)}\bar v(\nu,\mechanical{q_2})\gamma^\nu(1-\gamma_5)u(\nu,\mechanical{q_1})[\epsilon_+^*\cdot\epsilon_-^*(\mechanical{k_-}-\mechanical{k_+})_\nu\\
&\quad+\mechanical{k_+}\cdot\epsilon_-^*\,\epsilon_{+\nu}^*-\mechanical{k_-}\cdot\epsilon_+^*\,\epsilon_{-\nu}^*+\epsilon_-^*\cdot\mechanical{S}\;\epsilon_{+\nu}^*-\epsilon_+^*\cdot\mechanical{S}\;\epsilon_{-\nu}^*].
\end{aligned} \tag{6.3.70}
$$

<!-- PDF page 147; printed page 133 -->

![[gauge-theories-fig-6-16-z-pole-w-production.svg|**Fig. 6.16** $Z^0$-pole contribution to the reaction $\nu_e\bar\nu_e\rightarrow W^+W^-$ in the $\mathrm{SU}(2)_L\otimes\mathrm{U}(1)_Y$ theory.]]

On substituting the asymptotic forms (6.2.31) of the polarization vectors for the longitudinally polarized intermediate bosons and using

$$
\mechanical{k_+}\cdot\mechanical{S}=\mechanical{k_-}\cdot\mechanical{S}=\frac{\mechanical{S}^2}2, \tag{6.3.71}
$$

we find that

$$
\mathcal M_Z=\frac{ig}{8\mechanical{M_W}^2}\bar v(\nu,\mechanical{q_2})(\mechanical{\not\!k_+}-\mechanical{\not\!k_-})(1-\gamma_5)u(\nu,\mechanical{q_1}), \tag{6.3.72}
$$

for $\mechanical{S}^2\gg\mechanical{M_W}^2$. Then, by virtue of (6.2.34) and (6.2.35) and the definition (6.2.26), we have

$$
\begin{aligned}
\mathcal M_Z&=-\frac{ig^2}{4\mechanical{M_W}^2}\bar v(\nu,\mechanical{q_2})\mechanical{\not\!P}(1-\gamma_5)u(\nu,\mechanical{q_1})\\
&=-iG_F\sqrt2\bar v(\nu,\mechanical{q_2})\mechanical{\not\!P}(1-\gamma_5)u(\nu,\mechanical{q_1}),
\end{aligned} \tag{6.3.73}
$$

which is to be added to the contribution of the electron-exchange graph of figure 6.9,

$$
\mathcal M_e=iG_F\sqrt2\bar v(\nu,\mechanical{q_2})\mechanical{\not\!P}(1-\gamma_5)u(\nu,\mechanical{q_1}). \tag{6.2.37}
$$

The sum vanishes. Thus, the $p$-wave $s$-channel resonance, the $Z^0$, has canceled the divergence in the $p$-wave scattering amplitude, and the amplitude is “asymptotically safe.”

Measurement of the cross section for the reaction

$$
e^+e^-\rightarrow W^+W^-, \tag{6.3.74}
$$

<!-- PDF page 148; printed page 134 -->

![[gauge-theories-fig-6-17-electron-w-pair-gauge-cancellation.svg|**Fig. 6.17** Diagrams that participate in the gauge cancellation in the reaction $e^+e^-\rightarrow W^+W^-$.]]

for which similar divergence cancellations occur, has been advocated [12] as a probe of the gauge structure of the electroweak theory. Neglecting the electron mass, this reaction is described by the three Feynman graphs in figure 6.17. The leading divergence in the neutrino-exchange diagram of figure 6.17(a) is canceled by the contributions of the direct-channel $\gamma$- and $Z^0$-exchange diagrams, figure 6.17(b) and (c), in complete analogy with the two graphs (figures 6.9 and 6.16) for $\nu\bar\nu\rightarrow W^+W^-$. We may verify the cancellation without carrying out the full calculation as follows. When fermion masses are neglected, the lepton-exchange diagrams of figures 6.9 and 6.17(a) represent equal amplitudes. In the very high energy approximation in which the $\gamma$ and $Z^0$ propagators are equal, it is easy to show that the sum of the contributions of diagram 6.17(b) and (c) is identical with the amplitude due to figure 6.16. The connection to the local gauge symmetry of the theory is easily traced.

A noteworthy achievement of the LEP experiments is the validation of the $\mathrm{SU}(2)_L\otimes\mathrm{U}(1)_Y$ symmetry for the interaction of gauge bosons with fermions and gauge bosons with gauge bosons in $e^+e^-\rightarrow W^+W^-$. This reaction is described by three Feynman diagrams that correspond to $s$-channel photon and $Z^0$ exchange and $t$-channel neutrino exchange. For the production of longitudinally polarized $W$-bosons, each diagram of figure 6.17 leads to a $J=1$ partial-wave amplitude that grows as the square of the c.m. energy, but the gauge symmetry enforces a pattern of cooperation. The contributions of the direct-channel $\gamma$- and $Z^0$-exchange diagrams cancel the leading divergence in the $J=1$ partial-wave amplitude of the neutrino-exchange diagram. The interplay is shown in figure 6.18. If the $Z$-exchange contribution is omitted (middle line) or if both the $\gamma$- and $Z$-exchange contributions are omitted (upper line), the calculated cross section grows unacceptably with energy. The measurements compiled by the LEP Electroweak Working Group [13] agree well with the benign high-energy behavior predicted by the full electroweak theory.

Hadron collider measurements do not directly determine the $W^+W^-$ invariant mass because of the missing energy carried by neutrinos, but they reach beyond

**Fig. 6.18** Cross section for the reaction $e^+e^-\rightarrow W^+W^-$ measured by the four LEP experiments, together with the full electroweak-theory simulation (solid line) and cross sections that would result from $\nu$-exchange alone (dots) and from $(\nu+\gamma)$ exchange (dashes). (From Ref. [13].)

> [!note]
> This plot uses approximate curve and measurement values read from the printed figure. The source does not supply a numerical data table. The original plot below preserves all source measurements and error bars.

![[gauge-theories-fig-6-18-lep-w-pair-source.png|**Fig. 6.18** Original source plot. Cross section for the reaction $e^+e^-\rightarrow W^+W^-$ measured by the four LEP experiments, together with the full electroweak-theory simulation (solid line) and cross sections that would result from $\nu$-exchange alone (dots) and from $(\nu+\gamma)$ exchange (dashes). (From Ref. [13].)]]

the highest energy studied at LEP. The latest contributions, from the D0 [14] and CDF [15] collaborations, are in agreement with standard-model expectations [16] and tighten the bounds on anomalous couplings. The ATLAS and CMS experiments at the Large Hadron Collider will probe with increased sensitivity in $pp$ collisions at $\sqrt s=7\,\unit{TeV}$ and beyond [17].

This beautiful experimental validation of “gauge cancellation” is not the end of the story, however. As we shall see in section 6.6 and problem 6.12, an unacceptable growth with energy persists when a nonzero electron mass is restored. This is canceled not by the gauge symmetry, but by the contribution of the Higgs boson.

## Physical quantities

| Symbol | Unit | Quantity | Description |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| $\mathsf L,\mathsf L_e,\mathsf R,\mathsf R_e$ | $\unit{GeV^{3/2}}$ | Lepton multiplets | The left-handed doublet and right-handed singlet fields. |
| $e,e_L,e_R,\nu,\nu_L,\nu_R$ | $\unit{GeV^{3/2}}$ | Lepton fields | The electron and neutrino fields and their chiral parts. |
| $I,I_3,Y,Y_L,Y_R,Y_\phi$ | $1$ | Weak quantum numbers | Weak isospin, its projection, and weak hypercharge. |
| $\electric{Q}$ | $1$ | Electric charge number | The electric charge in units of the elementary charge. |
| $\electric{e},\electric{q}$ | $1$ | Electric charge | The electromagnetic coupling and electron charge, in natural units. |
| $g,g'$ | $1$ | Gauge couplings | The weak-isospin and weak-hypercharge couplings. |
| $\alpha,\alpha_2,\alpha_Y$ | $1$ | Interaction strengths | The electromagnetic, weak-isospin, and hypercharge coupling parameters. |
| $b_\mu^l,\mathbf b_\mu,\mathcal A_\mu$ | $\unit{GeV}$ | Gauge fields before mixing | The weak-isospin and hypercharge vector fields. |
| $W_\mu^\pm,Z_\mu,\magnetic{A_\mu}$ | $\unit{GeV}$ | Physical gauge fields | The charged and neutral weak fields and the photon field. |
| $F_{\mu\nu}^l,f_{\mu\nu}$ | $\unit{GeV^2}$ | Gauge field strengths | The field-strength tensors of the two gauge groups. |
| $\mathcal L$ and its sector terms | $\unit{GeV^4}$ | Lagrangian densities | The gauge, lepton, scalar, and Yukawa terms of the theory. |
| $\phi,\phi^+,\phi^0$ | $\unit{GeV}$ | Scalar doublet | The charged and neutral components of the Higgs doublet. |
| $\eta,\boldsymbol\zeta$ | $\unit{GeV}$ | Scalar excitations | The physical Higgs field and the would-be Goldstone fields. |
| $\energy{V}$ | $\unit{GeV^4}$ | Scalar potential density | The potential for the Higgs doublet. |
| $\mechanical{\mu}^2$ | $\unit{GeV^2}$ | Scalar mass parameter | The coefficient of the quadratic scalar potential. |
| $\lambda,\zeta_e$ | $1$ | Scalar and Yukawa couplings | The scalar self-coupling and the electron Yukawa coupling. |
| $v,\langle\phi\rangle_0,\langle\phi^0\rangle_0$ | $\unit{GeV}$ | Vacuum expectation values | The electroweak scale and the scalar vacuum values. |
| $\mechanical{M_W},\mechanical{M_{W^\pm}},\mechanical{M_Z},\mechanical{M_{Z^0}},\mechanical{M_H}$ | $\unit{GeV}$ | Boson masses | The charged weak, neutral weak, and Higgs masses. |
| $\mechanical{m_e}$ | $\unit{GeV}$ | Electron mass | The mass generated by the electron Yukawa interaction. |
| $\theta_W$ | $1$ | Weak mixing angle | The mixing angle of the neutral gauge fields. |
| $G_F$ | $\unit{GeV^{-2}}$ | Fermi constant | The strength of the low-energy charged-current interaction. |
| $\Gamma$ | $\unit{GeV}$ | Partial decay width | The leptonic decay width, with $\hbar=1$. |
| $L_e,R_e$ | $1$ | Chiral neutral-current couplings | The electron's left-handed and right-handed couplings to the Z boson. |
| $\mechanical{p},\mechanical{q},\mechanical{k},\mechanical{q_1},\mechanical{q_2},\mechanical{k_+},\mechanical{k_-},\mechanical{P},\mechanical{S}$ | $\unit{GeV}$ | Four-momenta | The external, internal, and total momenta in the displayed diagrams. |
| $s$ | $\unit{GeV^2}$ | Squared invariant energy | The squared total centre-of-mass energy. |
| $\epsilon_\pm^\mu$ | $1$ | Polarization vectors | The outgoing charged-boson polarization states. |
| $\mathcal M,\mathcal M_Z,\mathcal M_e$ | — | Reaction amplitudes | The decay or scattering amplitude and its separate contributions. |
| $u,\bar v$ | $\unit{GeV^{1/2}}$ | External spinors | The normalized neutrino and antineutrino spinors. |
| $\sigma_{WW}$ | $\unit{pb}$ | W-pair cross section | The W-pair production cross section shown in figure 6.18. |
| $J$ | $1$ | Angular momentum quantum number | The recoil-nucleus spin or the partial-wave angular momentum. |
| $\mechanical{\hbar}$ | $\unit{J\,s}$ | Reduced Planck constant | The constant used in the definitions of interaction strengths. |
| $\mechanical{c}$ | $\unit{m\,s^{-1}}$ | Speed of light | The constant used in the definitions of interaction strengths. |
